B routine priorityPublic-health news

High-Risk HPV Genotypes Not Covered by Vaccines Dominate Cervical Infections in Lagos Women

News Brief
A study conducted in Lagos, Nigeria, between July 2017 and June 2018 revealed that high-risk HPV genotypes not covered by vaccines, particularly HPV-35, dominate cervical infections among 165 sexually active women. The research, published in BMC Infectious Diseases, found that 43.6% of participants tested positive for HPV-35, which is more prevalent in this population than in European and North American studies. Additionally, 34.5% of women had multiple high-risk HPV infections, highlighting the limitations of current screening methods. The study, led by Oluwaseyi Sedowhe Ashaka of Caleb University, underscores the need for broader molecular testing and a reevaluation of vaccine strategies in regions with distinct HPV genotype distributions. The findings suggest that HPV-35 plays a significant role in cervical disease progression, appearing in all cytological grades from normal to high-grade lesions.
Original source text (English)
HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER Friday, October 16, 2026 No Result View All Result Login HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER No Result View All Result No Result View All Result Home NEWS Science News Health High-Risk HPV Genotypes Not Covered by Vaccines Dominate Cervical Infections in Lagos Women by October 14, 2026 in Health Reading Time: 6 mins read 0 High-Risk HPV Genotypes Not Covered by Vaccines Dominate Cervical Infections in Lagos Women Share on FacebookShare on TwitterShare on LinkedinShare on RedditShare on Telegram Cervical cancer remains one of the most formidable challenges in global oncology, ranking as the fourth most common cancer among women worldwide, and its primary driver is persistent infection with high-risk types of human papillomavirus. A new study conducted in Lagos, Nigeria, has now revealed a striking picture of which viral genotypes are actually circulating in this West African population, and the results carry uncomfortable implications for current vaccination strategies. Among 165 sexually active women recruited from a tertiary gynecology referral clinic, more than half carried at least one of the thirteen established high-risk HPV genotypes, and when five probable high-risk types were added to the analysis, the proportion of infected women climbed to just over seventy percent. The most frequently detected genotype was HPV-35, a type that is not fully covered by the vaccines most widely deployed in global immunization programs, a finding that the study’s authors argue should prompt a fundamental rethinking of region-specific vaccine design. The research, published in BMC Infectious Diseases, was led by Oluwaseyi Sedowhe Ashaka of Caleb University in Lagos together with collaborators spanning Nigerian institutions including the Nigeria Centre for Disease Control and Prevention, Lagos University Teaching Hospital, the Nigerian Institute of Medical Research, and the University of Ilorin, along with partners at The University of Texas Rio Grande Valley in the United States. Recruitment took place between July 2017 and June 2018, and every participant underwent both conventional cervical cytology screening by Pap smear and molecular testing for HPV. The dual approach allowed the investigators to compare viral genotype distribution in women whose cervical cells appeared normal under the microscope with those whose smears showed abnormalities ranging from atypical squamous cells of undetermined significance to low-grade and high-grade squamous intraepithelial lesions, the cytological precursors of invasive cancer. The molecular methodology at the heart of the study deserves particular attention because it goes beyond the detection limits of many routine screening assays. Rather than relying on a single amplification reaction, the team employed nested and multiplex polymerase chain reaction, a technique that uses two successive rounds of primer binding to boost sensitivity and then simultaneously amplifies genetic targets from eighteen distinct high-risk or probably high-risk HPV genotypes in the same reaction vessel. This nested multiplex PCR approach targeted thirteen established carcinogenic types, including the notorious HPV-16 and HPV-18, as well as five probable high-risk types such as HPV-82 and HPV-73, which are less commonly assayed in commercial test panels. The inclusion of these less-characterized genotypes proved consequential: HPV-82 was the second most prevalent type in the cohort at twenty percent, and HPV-73 ranked third at just over nine percent, figures that would likely have been missed by narrower testing strategies. The headline genotype, HPV-35, was detected in 43.6 percent of the women tested, an extraordinarily high proportion that dwarfs the frequencies typically reported for this type in European and North American surveillance studies. HPV-35 belongs to the alpha-9 species of papillomaviruses, the same phylogenetic group as HPV-16, and it expresses the E6 and E7 oncoproteins that interfere with the p53 and retinoblastoma tumor suppressor pathways, the canonical molecular mechanism by which high-risk HPVs drive malignant transformation. Crucially, HPV-35 was the most frequent genotype across every cytological grade in the study, meaning it appeared not only in women with normal smears but also in those with progressively severe epithelial abnormalities. This pattern suggests that HPV-35 is not a benign passenger in this population but an active participant in the spectrum of cervical disease, consistent with accumulating evidence from other sub-Saharan African settings where non-16 and non-18 types carry a disproportionate share of the oncogenic burden. Another notable finding was the frequency of mixed infections. Dual and multiple high-risk HPV infections were present in 34.5 percent of all participants, meaning roughly one in three women harbored two or more oncogenic genotypes simultaneously. Co-infection is biologically significant for several reasons. Multiple genotype infections can complicate viral competition and interference dynamics, may accelerate the progression of cervical lesions through additive or synergistic effects of different oncoproteins, and complicate genotype-specific surveillance because the presence of one dominant type can mask the detection of others depending on the assay used. In a clinical context, the high rate of multiple infections in Lagos underscores the limitations of screening algorithms designed around single-genotype detection and reinforces the case for broad-spectrum molecular panels in routine diagnostics. The cytological findings provided an important clinical anchor for the molecular data. Abnormal cervical cytology was identified in 21.8 percent of the cohort, a substantial proportion reflecting the fact that participants were drawn from a gynecology referral clinic rather than a general population sample. Within this abnormal group, HPV-35 again emerged as the most frequent genotype across all grades of dysplasia, from low-grade squamous intraepithelial lesions to high-grade lesions. The persistence of a single non-vaccine genotype across the full cytological spectrum raises pointed questions about how well current screening and vaccination paradigms, which were largely calibrated on the epidemiology of HPV-16 and HPV-18, translate to populations where the genotype distribution is fundamentally different. Beyond mapping the viral landscape, the study interrogated the behavioral and demographic risk factors associated with infection, using chi-square tests for initial comparisons and then multivariable logistic regression with Firth’s penalized-likelihood method to identify independent associations. This statistical approach is specifically designed for datasets in which some outcome categories contain few events, producing more reliable odds ratio estimates than standard maximum-likelihood regression when the data are sparse. Two factors emerged as independently associated with high-risk HPV infection after adjustment. A history of sexually transmitted infection nearly tripled the odds of carrying high-risk HPV, with an adjusted odds ratio of 3.27, while condom use was strongly protective, reducing the odds by more than eighty percent with an adjusted odds ratio of 0.19. Both associations were statistically robust, with confidence intervals that excluded the null value. Equally instructive were the factors that did not retain statistical significance. Later sexual debut, defined in the study as first intercourse at age twenty-one or older, and a higher number of lifetime sexual partners both showed elevated odds ratios in the initial analysis, but these did not reach statistical significance after adjustment. Similarly, age, educational status, and marital status were not independently associated with infection once other variables were accounted for. This pattern suggests that in this Lagos cohort, the dynamics of viral transmission and clearance are shaped more directly by sexual behavior variables with clear mechanistic links to exposure, such as barrier protection and prior sexually transmitted infections indicating unprotected exposure, than by broader demographic categories. The strong protective effect of condom use aligns with the established biology of HPV transmission, which occurs through direct contact with infected epithelial surfaces, and it offers an immediately actionable target for public health messaging even in settings where vaccine coverage remains low. The implications of the study extend well beyond Lagos. Most cervical cancer prevention programs worldwide rest on two pillars: vaccination against HPV-16 and HPV-18, the types responsible for the majority of cervical cancers globally, and cytological or molecular screening to detect precancerous lesions. The bivalent, quadrivalent, and nonavalent vaccines differ in the number of types they cover, with the nonavalent product adding HPV-31, 33, 45, 52, and 58 to the original pair, but none provides complete protection against HPV-35, HPV-73, or HPV-82. In a population where HPV-35 alone accounts for over forty percent of infections and the majority of detected genotypes fall outside even the broadest current vaccine formulation, the protective ceiling of existing immunization strategies is inherently limited. The authors argue that this mismatch makes a compelling case for region-specific vaccine development, potentially incorporating the genotypes that dominate African epidemiology, and for integrating molecular HPV genotyping into routine screening programs so that women carrying non-vaccine high-risk types are not falsely reassured. The study also highlights the practical infrastructure needed to act on such findings. The molecular work relied on positive controls supplied by the WHO HPV LabNet laboratory for the African region at the University of Cape Town, illustrating the value of international laboratory networks in supporting genotype-level surveillance where commercial assay coverage is incomplete. With cervical cancer incidence and mortality in sub-Saharan Africa among the highest in the world, and screening coverage in many countries remaining far below the targets set by the World Health Organization’s global elimination strategy, data of this granularity are essential for calibrating local responses. The Lagos findings, derived from a carefully genotyped cohort with both normal and abnormal cytology, provide a template for the kind of evidence base that vaccine developers, screening program designers, and policymakers will need if the goal of eliminating cervical cancer as a public health problem is to be achieved on a timeline that matters for the generations of women currently at risk. 为什么大多数 HPV 感染会消失,而少数感染却会致命:新的人工智能模型旨在预测差异

Why Most HPV Infections Vanish While a Few Turn Deadly: New AI Models Aim to Predict the Difference
新闻简报这篇文章讨论了列日大学研究人员开发的新人工智能模型,用于预测哪些 人乳头瘤病毒 (HPV) 感染会自行消退,哪些可能会发展为癌症。HPV 很普遍,大多数性活跃的成年人都会遇到至少一种病毒株,但只有一小部分会导致恶性肿瘤。挑战在于确定决定感染结果的因素,因为之前寻找单一生物标志物的努力已经失败。研究人员认为,HPV 驱动的致癌作用的复杂性涉及多种生物因素,包括病毒特征、免疫应答、遗传和表观遗传变化、微生物群、激素和环境影响。他们提出了一种系统级方法,集成了不同的数据类型,例如基因表达、表观遗传修饰和免疫特征,以创建更准确的预测模型。虽然这些模型显示出前景,但尚未经过临床使用验证,需要进一步测试、标准化和监管批准。潜在的好处包括更加个性化的风险评估和改进 HPV 相关癌症(如宫颈癌、口咽癌和肛门生殖器癌)的筛查策略。

Why Most HPV Infections Vanish While a Few Turn Deadly: New AI Models Aim to Predict the Difference

News Brief
The article discusses new AI models developed by researchers at the University of Liège to predict which human papillomavirus (HPV) infections will resolve on their own and which may progress to cancer. HPV is widespread, with most sexually active adults encountering at least one strain, yet only a small fraction leads to malignancy. The challenge lies in identifying the factors that determine infection outcomes, as previous efforts to find a single biomarker have failed. The researchers argue that the complexity of HPV-driven carcinogenesis involves multiple biological factors, including viral characteristics, immune response, genetic and epigenetic changes, microbiota, hormones, and environmental influences. They propose a systems-level approach that integrates diverse data types, such as gene expression, epigenetic modification, and immune signatures, to create more accurate predictive models. While these models show promise, they have not yet been validated for clinical use and require further testing, standardization, and regulatory approval. The potential benefits include more personalized risk assessment and improved screening strategies for HPV-associated cancers, such as cervical, oropharyngeal, and anogenital cancers.
Original source text (English)
HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER Friday, October 16, 2026 No Result View All Result Login HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER No Result View All Result No Result View All Result Home NEWS Science News Cancer Why Most HPV Infections Vanish While a Few Turn Deadly: New AI Models Aim to Predict the Difference by October 16, 2026 in Cancer Reading Time: 4 mins read 0 Why Most HPV Infections Vanish While a Few Turn Deadly: New AI Models Aim to Predict the Difference Share on FacebookShare on TwitterShare on LinkedinShare on RedditShare on Telegram Human papillomavirus is so widespread that most sexually active adults will encounter at least one of its strains at some point in their lives, yet the overwhelming majority of these infections disappear without a trace, cleared silently by the immune system within a year or two. The clinical dilemma lies in the small fraction of cases where the virus refuses to leave. When infection with a high-risk oncogenic HPV type persists, it can drive the slow transformation of cervical, oropharyngeal or anogenital tissue through precancerous stages and, in some individuals, all the way to invasive cancer. The question that has frustrated virologists and oncologists for decades is deceptively simple to state and extraordinarily difficult to answer: at the moment of diagnosis, which infections will resolve on their own and which are quietly marching toward malignancy? Researchers at the University of Liège have now confronted this question head-on in a review published in Trends in Molecular Medicine, arguing that the field’s long search for a single decisive biomarker may have been looking for the wrong kind of answer. Michael Herfs, an oncologist at the GIGA Institute in Liège, along with colleagues Diane Bruyère and Romane Geuzaine, contends that no viral, immune or molecular marker identified to date can reliably separate self-resolving infections from those destined to progress. Their analysis suggests the problem is not a shortage of candidate markers but a fundamental misunderstanding of what kind of biological system HPV-driven carcinogenesis actually is. The core of the difficulty is that HPV-associated cancer development is not a linear process with a single molecular switch. It is a dynamic, multi-layered phenomenon in which the outcome depends on the interplay of viral characteristics, the host’s immune response, genetic and epigenetic alterations, the local microbiota, hormonal influences and broader environmental factors. On top of these interacting variables sit stochastic events, most notably the accumulation of mutations driven by virus-induced genomic instability. Two patients infected with the same viral type can follow radically different trajectories because the determinants of progression are distributed across biological scales rather than concentrated in one measurable signal. This complexity helps explain why decades of biomarker research have produced incremental improvements rather than a decisive breakthrough. Viral load measurements, E6 and E7 oncogene expression, methylation patterns, immune cell profiling and a host of other candidate indicators have each shown promise in isolated studies, yet none has achieved the predictive accuracy needed to transform clinical decision-making. Herfs crystallizes the field’s predicament in a striking observation: perhaps the main challenge is no longer to discover a new biomarker, but to understand how all the biological mechanisms interact to determine the outcome of an HPV infection. The framing represents a conceptual shift from reductionist marker-hunting toward systems-level interpretation. It is precisely this shift that recent technological developments have made feasible. High-throughput molecular analysis platforms can now generate vast datasets capturing gene expression, epigenetic modification, viral integration sites and immune signatures simultaneously, while artificial intelligence and machine learning approaches offer the computational tools to integrate these heterogeneous data streams into coherent predictive models. Rather than asking whether a single marker predicts progression, integrative models can weigh dozens or hundreds of features at once, potentially capturing the multi-factorial nature of carcinogenesis that has defeated simpler approaches. The Liège team sees particular promise in using these models to distinguish between the possible trajectories of an infection or a low-grade precancerous lesion: spontaneous regression, persistence without progression, advancement to a more serious lesion, or evolution toward invasive cancer. Bruyère, a researcher at the Laboratory of Experimental Pathology, explains that artificial intelligence and integrative models could help clarify the diversity of biological trajectories observed in patients and ultimately enable a more personalised form of risk assessment. In practical terms, this would mean moving beyond the binary categorizations of current screening toward a graduated risk profile that reflects the actual biology of each individual infection. The clinical stakes of such a capability are considerable. Current management of HPV-positive screening results and low-grade lesions relies on HPV testing, cytology and histopathological analysis, supplemented by monitoring protocols that are only weakly personalised. This one-size-fits-all approach generates a well-documented burden of unnecessary colposcopies, repeat biopsies and anxiety-inducing follow-up for patients whose infections would have resolved regardless, while simultaneously risking under-surveillance in the minority whose lesions are genuinely dangerous. A validated predictive framework could tailor follow-up intensity to actual risk, reducing overtreatment and its associated costs and harms while concentrating clinical attention where it is most needed. However, the researchers are careful to temper expectations with an acknowledgment of the current gap between promise and practice. These new models have not yet been sufficiently validated for routine clinical use, and the path from an encouraging computational result to a deployable diagnostic involves rigorous external validation, standardization of input data, regulatory approval and demonstration of clinical utility across diverse populations. The history of biomarker research in oncology offers cautionary examples of tools that performed impressively in retrospective datasets but faltered in prospective clinical testing. The Liège authors position integrative AI approaches as the most promising avenue for improving risk stratification, not as a finished solution. Their broader conclusion reframes the research agenda for the field. Rather than continuing to pursue the elusive single indicator that will cleanly predict progression, the future lies in a better overall understanding of the biology of HPV infections as integrated biological systems. This perspective aligns with wider trends in cancer research, where multi-omics profiling and machine learning are increasingly applied to problems whose complexity resists traditional reductionist methods. For HPV specifically, the approach could eventually unify viral genomics, host immunology, epigenetics and microbiome data into models that reflect the true determinants of infection outcome. If that vision matures, the practical consequences could extend well beyond cervical cancer prevention. Persistent oncogenic HPV infection is also implicated in a rising proportion of oropharyngeal cancers and in anogenital malignancies, and improved risk prediction would benefit screening and surveillance strategies across all of these sites. For now, patients and clinicians must continue working with the imperfect tools of cytology, HPV genotyping and histopathology, but the Liège analysis offers a clear-eyed map of where the field must go next: away from the search for a molecular oracle and toward a genuinely integrative understanding of how viral and host biology combine to decide, infection by infection, whether the outcome is clearance or cancer. Fluorescent Makeover Gives HPV16 Virus-Like Particles a Self-Reporting Glow and Better Yield - Bioengineer.org

News Brief
Researchers at Texas Tech University have developed a method to fluorescently tag HPV16 virus-like particles (VLPs), enabling real-time monitoring of their assembly and reducing aggregation. This technique could improve the accessibility and yield of VLPs for research purposes, though its immunogenicity and in vivo functionality remain to be tested.
Original source text (English)
HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER Friday, October 16, 2026 No Result View All Result Login HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER No Result View All Result No Result View All Result Home NEWS Science News Health Fluorescent Makeover Gives HPV16 Virus-Like Particles a Self-Reporting Glow and Better Yield by October 16, 2026 in Health Reading Time: 5 mins read 0 Fluorescent Makeover Gives HPV16 Virus-Like Particles a Self-Reporting Glow and Better Yield Share on FacebookShare on TwitterShare on LinkedinShare on RedditShare on Telegram Human papillomavirus type 16 is best known as the leading cause of cervical cancer, but its hollow protein shell is also one of the most versatile tools in modern virology. Virus-like particles, or VLPs, built from the virus’s major capsid protein L1 are the backbone of every licensed HPV vaccine, and modified capsids are increasingly explored as delivery vehicles for drugs and genes aimed at tumor cells. A new study from Texas Tech University, published in Frontiers in Molecular Medicine, describes a way to rebuild HPV16 VLPs so that they glow when fully assembled, aggregate less during production, and can be made in ordinary laboratory cell lines without the specialized systems that current protocols require. The team, led by Morgan Williamson, Caleb Boren, Rahul Kumar and Lauren Gollahon, fused the two structural proteins of HPV16 to the two halves of a yellow fluorescent protein called Venus. The technique, known as bimolecular fluorescence complementation or BiFC, splits a fluorescent protein into an N-terminal and a C-terminal fragment, neither of which emits light on its own. When the two fragments are brought together by proteins that interact with each other, the fluorophore reconstitutes and fluoresces. By attaching the C-terminal half of Venus to L1 and the N-terminal half to L2, the researchers ensured that only particles containing both structural proteins would produce a detectable YFP signal, providing a built-in reporter of productive capsid assembly. A key design challenge was to attach the fluorophore halves without straining the capsid architecture. L1 assembles into pentamers that interlock through conserved disulfide bonds between the residues Cys175 and Cys428, forming an icosahedral shell roughly 50 to 60 nanometers in diameter. The native capsid contains approximately 360 copies of L1 along with 12 to 36 copies of the minor protein L2, which sits between pentamers and slightly tightens the shell. The researchers designed a specialized linker with the sequence HPPAPKED to join the fluorophore fragments to the capsid proteins, and structural predictions generated with AlphaFold2 and visualized in ChimeraX suggested the linker allowed the fluorophore halves to approach closely without obstructing the tertiary and quaternary contacts needed for assembly. Discover more Bioengineering News Subscription Bioengineering Career Guide Bioengineering Job Board iGEM Team Directory Bioengineering Events Calendar The constructs were built using overlap extension PCR, with the L1 and L2 coding sequences copied from the established p16sheLL expression vector and the Venus fragments taken from standard BiFC plasmids. Notably, the new coding regions were placed under a general CMV promoter rather than the SV40 large T-dependent system used in most published HPV16 VLP protocols, which achieves high plasmid copy numbers only in the specialized 293TT producer cell line. By using ordinary 293T cells instead, the authors argue, the platform becomes accessible to far more laboratories. The change also sidesteps recent concerns about producer cells expressing large T antigen, including the possibility of spontaneous packaging of large T coding sequences and the delivery of oncogenic DNA into healthy cells, a risk the team directly tested and did not observe. Expression of the fusion proteins matched theoretical predictions. Immunoblotting of transfected 293T cells detected L1 at around 60 kilodaltons and L2 at around 70 kilodaltons, consistent with the calculated molecular weights of the L1-VC155 and L2-VN173 fusion proteins. Flow cytometry tracked the BiFC signal over time and found peak intensity at approximately 72 hours after transfection, when roughly 17.6 percent of the total cell population produced detectable YFP, indicating successful co-uptake of both plasmids and intracellular capsid assembly. Live-cell imaging revealed the same cell swelling that earlier researchers famously described as producer cells filling up like beach balls, and the fluorescent signal was visible across multiple microscope platforms and filter sets, from dedicated YFP filters down to standard FITC channels. Particles were harvested by alkaline lysis with ammonium sulfate, which allows capsid maturation as disulfide bonds form between L1 subunits over 24 to 36 hours, and then purified on a discontinuous iodixanol density gradient by ultracentrifugation. The BiFC particles banded at the expected position, matching the density of the positive control p16sheLL particles, and transmission electron microscopy confirmed distinctive pentamer formation and icosahedral morphology. Dynamic light scattering placed the particles near 50 nanometers, in agreement with the electron micrographs, and showed less size variance than the conventional construct, which displayed multiple peaks including one near 4,000 nanometers corresponding to large aggregates. That reduction in aggregation is one of the study’s most practical findings. Conventional L1-only VLPs are notorious for clumping into mega-particle assemblies that complicate purification, measurement, and formulation, and the largest aggregate observed in the control samples spanned a full ten microns, roughly a thousand times the diameter of a single particle. In contrast, no mega-aggregates appeared in the BiFC preparations under either aqueous or dry conditions. The authors suggest that the linker and fluorophore slightly alter the electrostatic and hydrophobic surface interactions that drive mesoscale particles together, yielding more uniform suspensions and potentially a higher fraction of usable particles recovered after purification. Discover more Bioengineer Blog Access Bioengineering Research Papers Scientific science Science The fluorescent tag also opens a route to sorting. Because particles carrying both L1 and L2 differ from L1-only shells in fluorescence, overall charge, and estimated mass, the authors lay out several strategies for separating them. At physiological pH, BiFC-modified L1 carries a net positive charge of roughly plus 27 compared with minus 3 for unmodified L1, a difference that could be exploited with field-flow fractionation using a charged membrane and crossflow. The added fluorophore mass shifts total particle weight enough that the extreme populations, L1-only shells and fully loaded L1/L2 capsids, become distinguishable by resonant mass measurement, which gauges buoyant mass from the frequency shift of a vibrating resonator. The authors caution that the incremental weight difference per additional L2 molecule remains too small for conventional size-based separation, but the combination of charge, fluorescence, and mass could support high-throughput workflows in future flow virometry systems. Estimated yields were workable for laboratory use. Counting particles per unit area on electron microscopy grids, the team calculated roughly 33.59 times ten to the seventh particles per milliliter fraction, sufficient to achieve a multiplicity of infection of about 6.7 when dosing cell cultures, within the commonly recommended range of 0.1 to 10. Quality control analyses, including PCR screening of extracted capsid contents with primers for L1, L2, and SV40 large T sequences, found no detectable encapsidated DNA, confirming that the particles are empty shells. This emptiness is an important safety feature for a platform intended to serve as a foundation for packaging therapeutic genes or chemotherapeutic payloads for targeted delivery. The work remains at an early stage, and several questions are unresolved, including whether L1 and L2 fusion proteins made in separate cells can assemble during cell lysis, and whether the higher relative L2 expression in the new system saturates each particle with fluorophore and boosts per-particle brightness. Immunogenicity and in vivo functionality have yet to be tested. Still, the study establishes that a fluorescently self-reporting HPV16 capsid can be produced correctly, imaged on routine equipment, kept free of contaminating nucleic acid, and maintained in a less aggregated state than the current standard. If further studies confirm its immunological performance, the platform could support both next-generation vaccines aimed at broader cross-type protection through L2 epitopes and precision delivery vehicles that exploit the virus’s natural tropism for the heparan sulfate proteoglycans displayed on disturbed and cancerous cells, bringing together vaccine science, nanotechnology, and cancer therapy around a single glowing shell. Subject of Research: Fluorescently tagged HPV16 virus-like particles engineered for real-time assembly monitoring, reduced aggregation, and potential particle sorting Article Title: A novel approach to HPV16 VLP construction and production with improved accessibility and particle sorting potential Article References: Williamson, M., Boren, C., Kumar, R., & Gollahon, L. (2026). A novel approach to HPV16 VLP construction and production with improved accessibility and particle sorting potential. メインコンテンツへスキップ Chapter 2: The day I thought I might have an HPV infection 小夏 2026年10月15日 14:59 Table of Contents Sexual intercourse = infection? It is not something that only some people get infected with. No symptoms is just one piece of information to consider. If there are changes in your body... Let's break down your current anxiety into three parts 1⃣ What we know now 2⃣ Things we cannot know with current information 3⃣ What I can do for myself from now on For example, if you are in this situation right now When you look up HPV, you realize that men can get infected too, right? And, wanting to reassure yourself that you're okay, you looked up people with the same worries as you on AI or social media, or searched for symptoms. Even just one sexual encounter can lead to an HPV infection. From whom? Since when... Even if you don't know yet if you're infected, HPV is a virus that most people who have had sexual experience have been infected with. “I’m sure I’m infected too.” What’s really scary might not just be that you are infected... ・I wish I had known more back then → Regret ・If I gave it to her, is it my fault? → Impatience ・I don't want my parents to know I've had sexual intercourse → Embarrassment ・What will they ask me if I go to the hospital? → Fear/Anxiety Unable to ask anyone, infected or not infected you keep searching for the answer all by yourself. In this chapter, for those teenage boys, we will look at “Am I infected with HPV?” Instead of gathering scary information, let’s separate what we know now from what we don’t know yet. Sexual intercourse = infection? First of all, the possibility that you are infected immediately after having sex is not zero. Generally, it is said that 80–90% are infected. It is a virus transmitted through sexual intercourse and the like. When you hear that, you suddenly start worrying about your own body, but you don't need to get anxious all of a sudden! It is true that even if you are infected, there are no symptoms, and it is often only noticed after it has turned into cancer. There is no particular pain, and no itching. Even visually, you can't find anything wrong yourself. Even if a stranger tells you it's okay... it feels a bit strange, doesn't it. What are you searching for on AI or social media?? There is so much information, and looking for people in the same situation as me makes me scared or relieved, my emotions are constantly being swayed. Once you start worrying, the questions never stop. "Could I already be infected?" "I used a condom, but is it okay?" "The other person had no symptoms, so am I fine?" Conversely, even if I have no symptoms, is there a possibility I have passed it on to someone else? Before I knew it, my search history was all about HPV. Even if I read an article that makes me feel a bit relieved, the word "cancer" appearing in another article makes me scared again, doesn't it. Maybe it would be faster to ask someone. But, asking my parents is a very high hurdle... I can't really tell my friends or teachers either. That's why I worry alone, and consult AI... But in the end, I don't get a satisfying answer... I can't find anything that makes me feel truly at ease. ↑↑ I totally understand. You're searching because you can't easily talk about it, right. If you talk about HPV, I think you'll end up having to talk about the fact that you've had sexual intercourse. You don't want to talk about your delicate matters or things you want to keep hidden, right. I don't want to consult a friend and have weird rumors spread. I don't want to ask her and scare her more than necessary. So in the end, I keep looking at my smartphone alone. If you are in that state right now, let's organize one thing first! You don't need to be overly anxious just because you have had sexual intercourse! However, be careful not to judge for yourself that you are not infected just because you have no symptoms! Even if you don't know your infection status yet, there is no need to worry unnecessarily! This is the first important point.🌸 It is not something that only some people get infected with. The first thing you should know is that HPV is not a rare virus. It has been announced that almost everyone who has had sexual intercourse will be infected with HPV at some point in their lives. Here is a link with more detailed information.😊↓↓ About Genital HPV Infection This page answers basic questions about human papillomavirus www.cdc.gov If you have had sexual experience, such as sexual intercourse, it is a virus that could potentially affect anyone. Therefore, it is not a special, rare infectious disease. Moreover, even if the partner has no visible symptoms, even if you have only had one sexual partner, there is a possibility of being infected with HPV! So, when thinking about HPV, let's stop blaming ourselves or our partners. First, know the facts. That is where it starts. No symptoms is just one piece of information to consider. With an HPV infection, many people do not show symptoms or health problems. Most HPV infections disappear naturally, and about 9 out of 10 people will have it disappear naturally within two years. On the other hand, if some infections persist, they can lead to cases like condyloma acuminatum or certain types of cancer. In other words, no symptoms does not mean you are definitely not infected . At the same time, having had sexual intercourse does not mean you are infected with HPV either... It is truly complicated, but once you have had sexual intercourse, it is not overthinking to suspect the possibility of infection. When people get anxious, they inevitably want to know right away whether they are 'infected or not'💦 But no matter how many times you check your body yourself, or keep looking at search results, the answer you want will not appear. That is precisely why, Feeling anxious ↓ If I get tested, I will know everything ↓ If it is negative, I can be completely at ease before you think so simply, you need to know about HPV testing for men💡 I will organize this point in more detail in a later chapter. If there are changes in your body... If you feel something is wrong, please do not rely only on AI or social media to make a self-diagnosis. If you have concerning changes around your genitals, such as warts, growths, lumps, or sores that are hard to heal, that is a different story. Rather than searching for images and continuing to look for photos similar to your symptoms, you will definitely get a more accurate diagnosis by consulting a medical institution! In my case, I was actually infected with HPV and had a detailed examination, learned that it had progressed, and was able to detect it early and receive treatment just one step away from cancer! 'It looks like the photos on the internet, so it must be HPV.' 'It doesn't look like them, so it's not HPV.' Let's make sure not to diagnose it by yourself like that!! Let's break down your current anxiety into three parts Even after reading this far, 'But in the end, I still don't know if I'm infected' some of you may have thought so😣 That is true, This note is not intended to force things you don't understand into 'it's okay'. Rather, what is important is that I want you to be conscious of not blaming anyone, not trying to find peace of mind through searching, but also not leaving it alone . To that end, I will try to break down the anxiety you are currently feeling into three parts. 1⃣ What we know now Here, I will include not imagination, but only the facts that can be confirmed now instead. For example, ☘️Have or have not had sexual intercourse ☘️Used or did not use a condom ☘️Currently have or do not have concerning symptoms like pain or itching That is all for now👍! At this point, you don't need to imagine things you don't know yet, such as “I had sexual intercourse, so I have an HPV infection” or “I didn't use a condom, so I definitely have an infection.” First, draw a line at the facts that are happening to you right now . We will accept only the facts. 2⃣ Things we cannot know with current information Next, things that have no answer no matter how much you think about them now. ・Do I have an HPV infection? ・When was I infected? ・Who did I get it from? ・Was I infected during this sexual encounter? What I want to convey most here is that “I don't know” does not mean “a bad result” . For example, suppose you start from the state of “I don't know if I'm infected.” You might find yourself thinking, “I'm probably infected,” “Then I might have infected her too,” “What if she gets sick because of me?” But all you knew at the beginning was “I don't know if I'm infected” , right? When people get anxious, they tend to think negatively. Are you feeling like what should I do, what should I do💦? Let's put aside the things we don't know for now! There is no answer today! There won't be an answer tomorrow either🫣 3⃣ What I can do for myself from now on And the last thing to think about is, “So, what can I do for myself right now?” . 🚨 Learn reliable information about HPV. 🚨 Consult a medical institution if you have any concerning symptoms or changes. 🚨 Learn about prevention methods you can take from now on, such as the HPV vaccine or condoms. 🚨 Consult a professional if the need arises. Here, let's narrow it down to actions you can choose yourself! It's not about “making it 100% clear right now whether you are infected or not”💡 Instead of spending hours thinking about things you cannot solve yourself, you can think only about what you can do right now. For example, if you are in this situation right now “I had sexual intercourse with my partner last month. I don't have any particular symptoms right now. But I learned about HPV and got scared.” In this case, What is known → I have experienced sexual intercourse. I have no concerning symptoms right now. What is unknown → Whether I am infected with HPV, or when or from whom I might have been infected, cannot be determined with just the current information. What can be done → Learn correct information. Consult someone if there are any concerning changes. Think about prevention methods you can take from now on. Once you have organized it this far, don't you see what you need to do, like realizing you don't have to look for more evidence of infection tonight? That is the purpose of dividing things into these three categories, not to convince yourself that “everything is absolutely fine.” Do not take the unknown too negatively You cannot change the fact that you had sexual intercourse, and sometimes there is no answer right away. But just because of that, “I must be infected” “It's my fault” “It's too late” you don't have to take things in a bad direction yourself. Stop at what is known. Leave the unknown as it is. Then, choose one thing you can do yourself. If you can do this, even when anxiety arises, instead of continuing to search for answers online, you will be able to think, “What do I need right now?” instead. Conversely, be careful not to assume that everything is absolutely fine just because you have no symptoms. Separate what you know from what you don't know yet. Even just doing that can really help clear your head. And the next thing that comes up is if there are no symptoms, is it really okay? —that kind of anxiety. In the next chapter, I will explain 'asymptomatic HPV,' which is something teenage boys often find particularly confusing. 【楽天市場】がんリスク検査 N-NOSE エヌノーズ(ポストに提出・結果はWEBで閲覧が可能です)):N-NOSE公式楽天市場店 尿で全身23種のがんを網羅的に調べます CMでおなじみ♪ ポスト投函専用キット。がんリスク検査 N-NOSE エヌノーズ( a.r10.to ヒトパピローマウイルスとがん - 公益社団法人 日本WHO協会 ヒトパピローマウイルス (HPV) は、200 種類あるウイルスのグループ名です。 ほとんどの人に心配はありま japan-who.or.jp Human papillomavirus and cancer WHO fact sheet on human papilloma virus and cancer, including www.who.int Human Papillomavirus (HPV) Infection - STI Treatment Guidelines STI Treatment Guidelines from CDC www.cdc.gov #自分 #不安 #症状 #感染 #性交渉 #男性HPV #自分 #不安 #症状 #感染 #性交渉 #男性HPV トップ 健康・医療 病気 あなたへのおすすめ ピックアップされています 【10代男性のHPV】誰にも言えない自分の秘密 6本

BioNTech Provides Update on Phase 2/3 Clinical Trial for mRNA Cancer Immunotherapy Candidate BNT113 in Metastatic HPV16-positive Head and Neck Cancer - The Manila Times

Original source text (English)
AHEAD-MERIT Phase 2/3 clinical trial did not meet its first dual primary endpoint of progression-free survival for the mRNA cancer immunotherapy candidate BNT113 plus pembrolizumab compared with pembrolizumab alone - The trial will continue to evaluate the second dual primary endpoint of overall survival completing the assessment with the potential of this treatment approach to help patients live longer - Under the trial’s pre-specified statistical design, a statistically significant improvement in overall survival would establish a positive trial outcome, even if the progression-free survival dual primary endpoint was not met - mRNA cancer immunotherapies are a key pillar of BioNTech’s oncology strategy, which includes novel treatment combinations to maximize the potential of its diversified oncology pipeline and elevate treatment outcomes for patients MAINZ, Germany, October 13, 2026 - BioNTech SE (Nasdaq: BNTX, "BioNTech” or "the Company”) today announced the first topline results from the AHEAD-MERIT Phase 2/3 clinical trial (NCT04534205) for its investigational mRNA cancer immunotherapy BNT113 (tenumomeran/raplemomeran or "tenu-ra”). The clinical trial is evaluating BNT113 in combination with pembrolizumab compared to pembrolizumab monotherapy as a first-line treatment for patients with unresectable recurrent or metastatic human papillomavirus 16-positive ("HPV16+”) head and neck squamous cell carcinoma ("HNSCC”) expressing PD-L1. Approximately one third of HNSCC cases are associated with HPV infection. The majority are linked to HPV16, one of the most common, high-risk HPV types associated with cancer. The Phase 3 part of AHEAD-MERIT has dual primary endpoints, progression-free survival ("PFS”) and overall survival ("OS”), providing complementary measures of clinical benefit. At the analysis for PFS, BNT113 in combination with pembrolizumab did not demonstrate a statistically significant improvement in PFS versus pembrolizumab alone. The Independent Data Monitoring Committee ("IDMC”) has recommended that the clinical trial continues as planned to evaluate the second primary endpoint of OS to complete the assessment of the potential of this approach to help patients live longer. Under the trial’s pre-specified statistical design, a statistically significant improvement in overall survival would establish a positive trial outcome, even if the PFS dual primary endpoint was not met. BioNTech remains blinded to ongoing trial data in accordance with the trial’s governance procedures. "The potential of mRNA cancer immunotherapies in combination regimen lies in their ability to induce immune responses that may translate into durable clinical benefit and, ultimately, prolonged survival of patients. While the PFS result was not statistically significant at this first analysis, the data support continued evaluation of BNT113 plus pembrolizumab in this trial and show that it is engaging its intended therapeutic mechanism,” said Prof. Özlem Türeci, M.D., Co-Founder and Chief Medical Officer at BioNTech. "As immune-mediated anti-tumor effects build and deepen over time, the analysis of the second dual primary endpoint, overall survival, will be critical to fully assess the potential of this approach to deliver durable benefit for patients with HPV-driven head and neck cancer.” Despite the distinct characteristics of HPV-positive tumors, there are currently no HPV-targeted treatments approved. Many patients with HPV16+ HNSCC experience disease progression with current standard-of-care treatments, underscoring the continued need for novel HPV-targeted options that improve long-term survival. The investigational mRNA cancer immunotherapy BNT113 encodes a fixed set of antigens to specifically target two HPV16-derived antigens that are frequently expressed on HPV16+ tumor cells. BioNTech’s mRNA-based cancer immunotherapies are a key pillar of its oncology strategy, which includes novel treatment combinations to maximize the potential of its diversified oncology pipeline and elevate treatment outcomes for patients. About AHEAD-MERIT AHEAD-MERIT is an open-label, controlled, multi-site, interventional, 2-arm, Phase 2/3 trial of BNT113 in combination with pembrolizumab compared to pembrolizumab monotherapy as first line treatment in patients with unresectable recurrent or metastatic human papillomavirus 16-positive ("HPV16+”) head and neck squamous cell carcinoma ("HNSCC”) expressing programmed cell death ligand-1 (PD-L1) with combined positive score (CPS) ≥1. The trial has multiple parts: Part A, is an initial non-randomized Safety Run-In Phase to confirm the safety and tolerability at the selected dose range level of BNT113 in combination with pembrolizumab; Part B, is a randomized part to generate pivotal efficacy and safety data of BNT113 in combination with pembrolizumab versus pembrolizumab monotherapy in the first line setting in patients with unresectable recurrent or metastatic HPV16+ HNSCC expressing PD-L1 with CPS ≥1. Patients are treated with BNT113 in combination with pembrolizumab or with pembrolizumab monotherapy for up to approximately 24 months. Part B has two dual primary endpoints, progression-free survival ("PFS”) and overall survival ("OS”), providing complementary measures of clinical benefit. Secondary endpoints include safety, objective response rate ("ORR”) and duration of response ("DoR”). About BNT113 BNT113 (also known as tenumomeran/raplemomeran or "tenu-ra”) is an investigational mRNA cancer immunotherapy encoding the E6 and E7 proteins of HPV16, that are frequently found in HPV16+ solid tumors. This mRNA cancer immunotherapy approach is designed to induce HPV16-specific anti-tumor immune responses, thereby aiming to enhance clinical responses in patients being treated with checkpoint inhibitor standard of care treatment. BNT113 received Fast Track Designation by the U.S. Food and Drug Administration ("FDA”) in January 2026. About head and neck squamous cell carcinoma (HNSCC) HNSCC is the seventh most common cancer type worldwide with increasing global incidence, mainly driven by a rise in HPV16-related oropharyngeal tumors, the most common subtype of HNSCC.1,2 About one third of HNSCC cases are HPV-positive following HPV infection, with a rising trend, of which about 90% of oropharyngeal cancers are driven by the subtype HPV16.3,4 About BioNTech BioNTech is a global next generation biopharmaceutical company pioneering novel investigative therapies for cancer and other serious diseases. In oncology, BioNTech is committed to transforming how cancer is treated. Its ambition is to develop innovative medicines with pan-tumor or synergistic potential to address cancer from multiple angles and across the full continuum of the disease from early- to late-stage. Its growing late-stage oncology pipeline comprises complementary treatment approaches spanning immunomodulators, antibody drug conjugates, and mRNA cancer immunotherapies. BioNTech has partnered with multiple global and specialized pharmaceutical collaborators leveraging complementary expertise and resources to accelerate innovation and drive progress, including Bristol Myers Squibb, Duality Biologics, Genentech, a member of the Roche Group, Genmab, MediLink, OncoC4, and Pfizer. For more information, please visit www.BioNTech.com. 地点与对象
在随访期间,队列研究 积累了一组有意义的终点:2,926 名女性被诊断患有 3 级宫颈上皮内瘤变,或 CIN3,即主要的鳞状前体病变;174 发展为原位腺癌,相当于腺癌;66人被诊断患有浸润性鳞状细胞癌;23 名患有侵袭性腺癌。
事件
A reported history of chlamydia infection carried a hazard ratio of 1.43 (95 percent CI: 1.31 to 1.56).
规模、影响与风险
HPV 疫苗接种 可预防大多数 宫颈癌 的高风险类型,在四价和非价 疫苗 中,还可预防引起疣的 6 型和 11 型,仍然是这两种途径最强大的预防工具。
应对、状态与不确定性
随着 疫苗接种 计划的成熟和筛查技术转向 HPV 检测,了解哪些辅助因素驱动哪种病变类型对于设计下一代预防策略至关重要。

HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER Friday, October 16, 2026 No Result View All Result Login HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER No Result View All Result No Result View All Result Home NEWS Science News Health Smoking, Contraception and Sexual History Shape Distinct Cervical Cancer Risks in 117,000-Women Study by October 15, 2026 in Health Reading Time: 5 mins read 0 Smoking, Contraception and Sexual History Shape Distinct Cervical Cancer Risks in 117,000-Women Study Share on FacebookShare on TwitterShare on LinkedinShare on RedditShare on Telegram Cervical cancer has long been framed as a single disease with a single cause: persistent infection with human papillomavirus, or HPV. A new Nordic study, however, adds weight to a growing body of evidence that the two main histological types of cervical neoplasia — squamous cell lesions arising from the outer surface of the cervix and glandular lesions arising from the cells lining the cervical canal — carry strikingly different lifestyle risk profiles. The distinction matters because while population-wide screening has driven squamous cell carcinoma rates sharply downward in high-income countries, adenocarcinoma and its precursor, adenocarcinoma in situ, have been quietly rising, particularly among younger women, and are harder to detect with conventional cytology. The research, published in BMC Public Health, is a prospective cohort study led by Madleen Orumaa of the Cancer Registry of Norway together with collaborators from the Danish Cancer Institute, Karolinska Institutet in Sweden, and the Icelandic Cancer Registry. The team analyzed data from 117,511 women aged 18 to 45 in Norway, Sweden, Denmark and Iceland who completed detailed questionnaires about cervical cancer risk factors, including sexual history, contraceptive use, smoking, and prior sexually transmitted infections. The women were then followed for a mean of 9.5 years through national health registries, allowing the researchers to link self-reported lifestyle information collected before diagnosis with subsequent outcomes. During follow-up, the cohort accumulated a meaningful set of endpoints: 2,926 women were diagnosed with cervical intraepithelial neoplasia grade 3, or CIN3, the principal squamous precursor lesion; 174 developed adenocarcinoma in situ, the glandular equivalent; 66 were diagnosed with invasive squamous cell carcinoma; and 23 with invasive adenocarcinoma. Because glandular lesions are comparatively rare, the absolute numbers are small, and the authors themselves frame the findings as an important first step rather than a definitive answer. Even so, the prospective design — recording exposures before any diagnosis — gives the associations considerably more credibility than retrospective case-control studies, which are vulnerable to recall bias after a cancer diagnosis. Discover more Find Cancer Care See an OB-GYN cancer Cancer cancers The statistical analysis used multivariate Cox regression, a technique that estimates hazard ratios for each exposure while adjusting for all the others, thereby isolating the independent contribution of each lifestyle factor. The results split cleanly along histological lines. For the squamous pathway — CIN3 and squamous cell carcinoma combined — four factors emerged as independently associated with risk. Each additional lifetime sexual partner raised the hazard by roughly 47 percent, with an adjusted hazard ratio of 1.47 and a 95 percent confidence interval of 1.34 to 1.56. A reported history of chlamydia infection carried a hazard ratio of 1.43 (95 percent CI: 1.31 to 1.56). Use of hormonal contraceptives was associated with a hazard ratio of 1.23 (95 percent CI: 1.02 to 1.47), and smoking added a small but measurable increment of 2 percent per pack-year (hazard ratio 1.02, 95 percent CI: 1.02 to 1.03). The glandular pathway told a different story. Women who went on to develop adenocarcinoma in situ or invasive adenocarcinoma did share some baseline characteristics with the squamous group — both groups reported an earlier age at first intercourse and a greater number of lifetime sexual partners compared with women who remained unaffected. But in the fully adjusted models, one factor stood out as specific to the glandular lesions: a history of genital warts, which are caused by low-risk HPV types 6 and 11, was associated with a hazard ratio of 1.60 (95 percent CI: 1.15 to 2.24) for AIS/AC. Notably, this association did not hold for the squamous lesions, suggesting that a visible history of warts may serve as a marker of a distinct pattern of HPV exposure or of individual susceptibility relevant to glandular transformation. Discover more Plan Your Family Support Causes Book a Checkup From a biological standpoint, the divergence is plausible. Squamous cells on the ectocervix and glandular cells in the endocervical canal are different cell types with different vulnerabilities to HPV-mediated transformation, and squamous and adenocarcinomas differ in their genomic profiles and their responses to treatment. The transformation zone, where squamous and glandular epithelia meet, is the epicenter of HPV-driven carcinogenesis, but the mechanisms by which co-factors such as smoking, hormonal exposure, and co-infections accelerate progression may not act identically in both cell lineages. Smoking, for example, is thought to act partly through local immunosuppression and the delivery of carcinogenic metabolites in cervical mucus, while hormonal contraceptives have been hypothesized to influence gene expression in HPV-infected cells. Chlamydia, meanwhile, may contribute through chronic inflammation that facilitates HPV persistence. The public health implications are considerable. The World Health Organization has set an ambitious global strategy to eliminate cervical cancer as a public health problem, built on three pillars: HPV vaccination, screening, and treatment of precancerous lesions. Most current screening programs were designed and validated in an era when squamous lesions dominated, and they rely primarily on cytology of the transformation zone. Glandular lesions, which sit higher in the endocervical canal and shed cells less readily, are notoriously under-detected by conventional Pap smears. The authors argue that their findings underscore the need for risk-based screening models — stratifying women by their individual risk profiles rather than applying a uniform interval to everyone — and for improving adherence to screening guidelines across both lesion types. Such risk stratification could, in principle, incorporate the factors identified in this study. A woman with a history of chlamydia, extensive smoking exposure, and multiple sexual partners might warrant closer surveillance for squamous lesions, while a history of genital warts might flag elevated glandular risk. HPV vaccination, which protects against the high-risk types responsible for most cervical cancers and, in the quadrivalent and nonavalent vaccines, against the wart-causing types 6 and 11 as well, remains the most powerful preventive tool for both pathways. The study’s finding that genital warts mark glandular risk adds an intriguing dimension: vaccination against types 6 and 11 may confer benefits beyond preventing the warts themselves, potentially reducing a marker-associated risk of glandular neoplasia. The authors are careful to note the limitations inherent in their design. The number of invasive adenocarcinoma cases — 23 — is small, which widens confidence intervals and limits the granularity of subgroup analyses. Lifestyle data were self-reported and therefore subject to misclassification, and the cohort comprised Nordic women aged 18 to 45 at enrollment, a relatively homogeneous, well-screened population whose findings may not generalize directly to settings with different screening coverage or HPV type distributions. The study was funded by Merck & Co. for questionnaire data collection and by the Nordic Cancer Union for manuscript preparation, with the sponsors having no role in data analysis or interpretation, according to the published disclosures. Even with those caveats, the study represents one of the largest prospective efforts to disentangle the lifestyle epidemiology of the two cervical cancer pathways, and it arrives at a moment when adenocarcinoma’s rising share of cervical cancer diagnoses is drawing increasing attention worldwide. As vaccination programs mature and screening technologies shift toward HPV testing, understanding which co-factors drive which lesion type will be essential for designing the next generation of prevention strategies. The Nordic registries, with their near-complete follow-up, offer an unusually clear window onto that question — and this study suggests the window reveals two distinct diseases, not one. Subject of Research: Lifestyle risk factors for glandular and squamous cervical neoplasia in a Nordic prospective cohort Article Title: Lifestyle risk factors for cervical glandular and squamous cell neoplasia: insights from a prospective cohort study of over 117,000 women Article References: Orumaa, M., Kjaer, S. K., Munk, C., Sundström, K., Tryggvadóttir, L., Campbell, S., Hetland Falkenthal, T. E., Nygård, S., & Nygård, M. (2026). Lifestyle risk factors for cervical glandular and squamous cell neoplasia: insights from a prospective cohort study of over 117,000 women.
